My entire career, my entire adult life, has been built on hard data. Zeros and ones. Binary realities. If I see something, it's because it is right there in black and white, printed on a physical server log or displayed on my monitor. I'm not... I'm not hallucinating phantom coworkers or hearing whispers in the air vents. Everything I am telling you is grounded in tangible, verifiable, digital evidence. \n\nNow, as for the last part of your question—believing something strongly that others find hard to understand—well, sure, but you have to look at the context. People find it \"hard to understand\" because they lack the technical literacy, not because what I'm saying is some kind of fantasy. Take my manager, for example. If you sat him down and tried to explain a simple SQL injection or how to configure a basic firewall rule, his eyes would glaze over. He wouldn't understand a word of it. So when I sit in his office and show him the timestamp discrepancies in the ticket routing, of course he doesn't agree with me! He doesn't have the baseline knowledge to agree or disagree. He just trusts whatever the dashboard tells him to trust. \n\nIt's like... imagine trying to explain advanced calculus to a toddler, and when the toddler doesn't get it, someone accuses you of making up the math. That's what it feels like. The fact that management or HR finds my claims \"hard to understand\" isn't a reflection of my mental state, doctor, it's a reflection of their complete ignorance of network architecture. So, no, no strange sensations, no voices. Just a very real, very documented administrative problem that no one else in that building is qualified to see.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period lasting several days or longer where your mood was unusually high, irritable, or you felt extremely energetic, where you needed less sleep, or were more focused on getting things done than usual?\n\nPatient: Unusually high? I haven't felt \"extremely energetic\" since the late nineties back when we were rolling out Windows 98 across three different corporate campuses. That took a lot of energy, let me tell you, we were working around the clock. But these days? No, my mood is... well, it's steady. I'm a steady, pragmatic kind of guy. \n\nAs for sleep, I absolutely need my sleep. I aim for a solid seven to eight hours every single night. Now, I will admit, lately, with everything going on at the office, with the constant need to double-check my ticket queues and document their little traps, I might toss and turn a bit. My wife will tell you I've been grinding my teeth, and sometimes it takes me an hour to wind down because my brain is still parsing through the day's server logs. But I'm not staying up for days on end, or feeling like I don't need to rest. I'm definitely not running on two hours of sleep. I leave that to the junior developers who survive on energy drinks and terrible coding habits. I know the value of pacing myself. \n\nAnd as for being more focused on getting things done... well, yes, I am focused, but not in some abnormal, unnatural way. It's just... when you're in my position, when you know there is a literal target on your back and a coordinated effort to push you out, you have to be focused. You have to dot every 'i' and cross every 't'. If I spend four uninterrupted hours meticulously reviewing a firewall configuration, it's not because I have some weird burst of energy, it's because if I miss one single parameter, they will use it as an excuse to write me up for a critical failure. So my focus is purely self-preservation, really. It's just dedication to my craft and protecting my livelihood from people who want to ruin it. So no, no strange mood swings or energy spikes. Just a guy trying to do his job, protect his reputation, and get a decent night's rest despite the circumstances.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period lasting two weeks or more where you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Down? Hopeless? No, I wouldn't use those words at all. I mean, sure, I get frustrated. Anyone would be frustrated when they’re dealing with the kind of systemic, targeted administrative lockouts I’m facing every single week. It’s aggravating, absolutely. You try trying to close out a tier-three support ticket when someone keeps remotely revoking your read-write permissions halfway through the process. But hopeless? Doctor, if I were hopeless, I would have just handed in my badge, packed up my desk, and let them win. I would have just rolled over and taken the early retirement package they keep trying to subtly push on me. \n\nBut I'm not giving up. I am actively fighting this. I'm documenting everything, I'm building a paper trail that even their highest-paid corporate lawyers won't be able to argue with. That takes drive. That takes determination, not hopelessness. \n\nAnd as for losing interest in things... no, not at all. When I’m not at the office, or, you know, when I’m not organizing my backup logs at home, I still do the things I’ve always loved. I have a pretty extensive home network setup in the basement—I run my own media server, I tinker with Raspberry Pi microcomputers, I’ve been getting into local home automation lately. Which, by the way, is a whole other can of worms when you start looking at the security protocols on these cheap smart devices, let me tell you. I have to isolate them on their own VLAN just so they don't phone home to some server overseas. But I enjoy that kind of problem-solving! \n\nAnd my wife and I, we still do our Sunday morning crosswords, we go to the farmer's market when the weather is nice... I still enjoy my life. It’s just that right now, a significant portion of my bandwidth—mentally speaking—has to be allocated to making sure my career isn’t dismantled by management. But that doesn't mean I'm sitting around crying or staring at the wall. I honestly don't have the time to be depressed. I have way too much work to do, both on the clock and off it, just to keep my head above water and prove exactly what they're doing to my system access. So, no, no two-week periods of feeling down or losing interest. I'm just highly occupied with defending myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief that you were absolutely certain was true, even when the people around you, including those who knew you well, strongly disagreed with you and provided clear evidence to the contrary?\n\nPatient: Belief I'm certain is true? Well, yes, but again, I have to challenge your phrasing there, doctor. \"Clear evidence to the contrary.\" That's... that's the crux of the whole issue right there. That is actually the entire reason I'm here in this office talking to you right now. \n\nFor over a year now—fourteen months, if we want to be exact, ever since the Q3 restructuring last year—I have been trying, practically begging, to get someone, anyone, to take this seriously. I am talking about the new ticket rotation system and these so-called \"random\" security audits they've been running. It is blindingly obvious to me what is happening. They are systematically using these audits, and the way they route the impossible, broken tickets specifically to my queue, to build a paper trail against me at work. It's a textbook constructive dismissal tactic. You flood the senior employee with unmanageable tasks, you audit their terminal constantly, and you document every minor, unavoidable delay as a performance failure so you can push them out without paying a proper severance. \n\nBut of course, when I bring this up, when I go to HR or my department head and lay it all out... they disagree. Strongly. Just like you said. They sit me down in these little glass-walled conference rooms, and they pull up the system logs on the projector, and they say, \"Look, it's just the new automated rotation. It's just policy. Everyone is getting audited, it's completely random.\" They point to the logs as their \"clear evidence to the contrary.\" \n\nBut doctor, I *know* how those logs are generated! I know how the routing algorithms are configured! I've been doing this for thirty-five years. A log only shows what the system parameters tell it to show. If you set the policy to disproportionately flag legacy accounts—like mine—then the log is going to look perfectly standard and random to an untrained HR rep, but it's completely skewed in reality. \n\nSo yes, I am absolutely certain this paper trail is being built to terminate me, and yes, management strongly disagrees and waves their little policy printouts in my face. But they aren't providing evidence to the contrary, they're just showing me the very tool they're using to do it! I just... I can't get anyone to look past their surface-level \"it's just policy\" excuse and see the actual, underlying architecture of what they're doing to me. It's maddening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever held a belief that others strongly disagreed with, but which you remained absolutely certain was true despite their disagreement and any evidence they provided?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I feel like we are going in circles here, because that is exactly what I just spent the last five minutes explaining to you. That is the entire reason I am sitting in this chair right now. I’m here because for over a year now—fourteen months, as I said—I’ve been trying to get someone, anyone with a shred of technical literacy, to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and these constant security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work. \n\nAnd yes, exactly to your point about people disagreeing and providing \"evidence,\" management keeps showing me logs and saying it’s just policy. They pull me into these meetings, they slide a printout across the table—a literal piece of paper, if you can believe it, who prints out server logs in this day and age?—and they tap their fingers on it and say, \"Look, the algorithm assigned this batch, it's just standard policy, everyone goes through it.\" They expect me to just nod and accept that as objective proof. \n\nBut I wrote half the scripts they used to use before this new system! I know the architecture. I know that if you tweak the load-balancing parameters even slightly on the backend, you can flood a specific user profile—mine—with all the corrupted, unresolvable legacy tickets while making it look completely automated to an auditor. So yes, I remain absolutely certain, because their so-called evidence is just a smokescreen. It’s a manipulated output designed to look like a neutral policy. I just don't know how else to explain it so that people will actually listen to the technical reality instead of just quoting the corporate handbook at me. It's like talking to a brick wall.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that events, objects, or the actions of others were specifically directed at you, even when there was no logical reason for it, outside of your workplace?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? You mean like... strangers on the street, or things happening in my neighborhood? No, no, definitely not. I mean, I'm a very rational person, doctor. I don't walk around thinking the universe revolves around me or that the cashier at the grocery store is somehow involved in a plot against me. When my wife and I go to the farmer's market, like I mentioned earlier, the guy who sells us the heirloom tomatoes—great guy, by the way, we talk about soil pH and irrigation setups for twenty minutes at a time—he's just selling tomatoes. He's not secretly sending signals or whatever it is you're implying. \n\nHonestly, I think that's the clearest proof I have that what's happening at the office is a real, documented, targeted campaign. Because if I were just... I don't know, paranoid or losing my grip on reality, wouldn't I think everyone was out to get me? Wouldn't I think the mailman was spying on my house? But I don't. Bob, my neighbor on the left, we borrow each other's lawn tools all the time. The mailman is just delivering the mail. When I go down to Micro Center to pick up parts for my home server, the staff there are just kids trying to make an hourly wage, they aren't doing anything directed at me. \n\nMy life outside the office is completely normal. Boring, even, in a good way! It's just... logical. Everything has a logical reason. The only place where things are happening that are specifically directed at me is at work, and that's because there *is* a logical reason for it there. The motive is financial. They want to push out a senior employee with thirty-five years of salary accrual without paying the proper severance package. That's business. It's dirty business, but it makes logical sense from their bottom line. Out in the real world? No, nobody cares about me. I'm just a guy buying tomatoes and ethernet cables. So no, I don't feel like things outside of work are directed at me at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief that was not related to work that you were absolutely sure was true, even when the people around you, including those who knew you well, strongly disagreed and provided clear evidence that it wasn't true?\n\nPatient: I mean... doctor, again, I feel like you're searching for a pattern that just doesn't exist outside of that office building. I feel like we're splitting hairs here. If you're asking if I'm just generally a stubborn guy who ignores facts in my day-to-day life, the answer is no. Absolutely not. \n\nI don't have these... these irrational, unshakeable beliefs about my personal life, because my personal life isn't being manipulated by a corporate restructuring team. I'm a very logical man. If someone shows me real evidence, I listen to it. \n\nJust to give you an example—because I really need you to understand how I operate normally—last month, my wife and I had a disagreement. I was absolutely certain that the new mesh router I set up in the den, which is an ASUS dual-band, beautiful piece of hardware, I was convinced it was causing interference with our smart thermostat. I was sure of it, because the timing lined up perfectly with when I installed it. But my wife, she's a smart woman, she pulled up the connection logs on her tablet and showed me that the drop-offs were actually happening on a completely different 2.4 gigahertz frequency, coming from the neighbor's security camera setup. And you know what I did? I looked at the data—real, untampered data, not the garbage HR tries to feed me—and I said, \"You're right, Helen. I was wrong.\" \n\nThat is how normal people operate in the real world! When someone I trust shows me actual, objective evidence, I change my mind. I don't just blindly cling to things. \n\nSo no, I don't have anything like what you're describing outside of work. My life at home, my hobbies, my marriage... it's all grounded in reality. The only reason I am so unshakeable about what is happening at the office is because I know the backend architecture of their systems, and I know exactly how they are faking the evidence against me. It’s the Q3 restructuring, doctor. That's the anomaly here. It's not some overarching flaw in my psychology, it is a specific, documented campaign by management to avoid paying my severance. Important? Like... what, like a prophet, or some kind of secret agent? No. No, absolutely not. I mean, look at me, doctor. I'm a sixty-year-old man who works in IT support. I'm just a guy who has spent thirty-five years making sure networks don't collapse under the weight of corporate incompetence. That's not a grand mission, that's just the daily grind. I've never thought I was Steve Jobs or some tech visionary. I'm a nuts-and-bolts guy. I write scripts, I patch servers, I make sure the SQL databases don't corrupt during the nightly backups. It's practical work. It's almost blue-collar work, just done in a rolling chair. 

And honestly, if you think about it, that just proves my point about what management is doing right now. If I thought I was some incredibly important, irreplaceable genius, I wouldn't be sitting here telling you they're trying to push me out, would I? I'd be telling you they couldn't survive without me! But I know exactly what I am to them. I'm a line item. I'm a senior employee with a high salary and a massive severance package waiting for me if they lay me off legally. 

I'm not important to them as a person or a visionary, I'm just an expensive liability on their Q3 spreadsheet that they want to erase using these fake security audits. I know my place in the world, doctor. It's right there in the server room. So no, no grand missions here. Just a regular guy trying to protect his hard-earned pension from a bunch of executives who think they can use an algorithm to cheat him out of it.

They just keep smiling, showing me the exact same sanitized logs, and acting like it's just routine policy. It's exhausting, frankly, trying to get anyone to look at the actual reality of the situation when they just hide behind \"company policy\" while actively setting me up to fail.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that people at work are intentionally trying to harm you, set you up, or conspire against you in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, \"harm\"... I mean, if you're asking if someone is going to wait for me in the parking lot with a baseball bat, no, I'm not saying that. I'm a rational person, doctor. But professionally? Absolutely. \"Set me up\" is exactly the right phrase. \n\nYou see, when you've been in IT as long as I have—and we're talking about the early days, back when we were manually configuring IPX/SPX protocols, long before everything was just a neat little cloud-based dashboard—you learn how to read the digital footprints people leave behind. You see the patterns. And the pattern here is, frankly, undeniable. \n\nThey aren't just... it's not just some passive dislike or office politics. It's a highly coordinated effort to document a false narrative about my competence. I've thought about this a lot, analyzing the timestamps on the tickets I get assigned. For instance, I'll get a batch of priority-one server outage tickets right at 4:55 PM on a Friday. Now, the automated algorithm is supposed to distribute those evenly across the senior support team, right? There are several of us. But if you look at the load balancing, I am consistently receiving the vast majority of the late-day criticals. And when I inevitably miss the SLA—the Service Level Agreement, which is our internal metric for response times—because it is physically impossible for one man to resolve multiple critical database failures simultaneously, what happens? It goes straight into my quarterly review. It gets flagged as a failure to meet basic performance metrics. \n\nSo, yes, they are setting me up. They want a documented, supposedly objective history of underperformance. And the most frustrating part of all of this, the part that really makes my blood boil, is that management just looks at these manufactured metrics and nods along. They are completely ignorant—or willfully blind—to the backend manipulation it takes to generate those numbers. I've tried explaining the routing parameters to them, I've tried showing them how a simple script can bypass the randomizer to target my specific user ID, but they just glaze over. They don't want to hear it, because the setup gives them exactly what they want: a neat, tidy paper trail to eventually force me out. So yes, I absolutely believe they are conspiring against me. I don't just believe it, I can see the architecture of it.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your workplace, do you feel that other people in your life, like friends, family, or strangers, are also conspiring against you or trying to harm you in some way?\n\nPatient: No, no, not at all. It's strictly... it's confined entirely to the office. My family, my friends... they have nothing to do with this. I mean, my wife, she's been incredibly supportive through all of this, even though I know it's hard for her to grasp the technical nuances of what they're doing to the server logs. We've been married for thirty-five years, and she knows me, she knows my work ethic. She sees how stressed I am when I get home, how I have to spend hours just documenting everything that happened that day, taking screenshots of the ticket queues before they can scrub them, because you have to have your own backups, you know? \n\nAnd strangers? No, why would a stranger care about my SLA metrics? This isn't some grand, global conspiracy, doctor. I'm not... I'm not crazy. I'm not sitting here thinking the mailman is tapping my phone or the barista at the coffee shop is poisoning my espresso. That's absurd. This is a highly localized, professionally motivated campaign to remove me from my position without having to pay out a severance package or deal with a wrongful termination suit. \n\nIt's all about the money and the corporate restructuring they've been hinting at since Q3 of last year. They want the older, higher-salaried guys out so they can bring in these kids fresh out of a six-week coding bootcamp who don't know the difference between a subnet mask and a MAC address, but who will work for half the pay and never question management. That's all this is. It's just business, but it's dirty business. So, no, my personal life is fine, or at least, as fine as it can be when you're dealing with this level of professional sabotage for eight to ten hours a day. It's just the people at the office. They're the ones with the motive, the means, and the administrative access to pull this off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any strange or unusual thoughts, sensations, or perceptions that other people don’t seem to notice or agree with, such as hearing voices, seeing things that others don’t, or believing something strongly that others find hard to understand?\n\nPatient: Hearing voices? Seeing things? No, no, let me stop you right there, doctor. Absolutely not. I think... I think you might be fundamentally misunderstanding the nature of what I'm dealing with here. \n\nI work in IT. My entire career, my entire adult life, has been built on hard data. Zeros and ones. Binary realities. If I see something, it's because it is right there in black and white, printed on a physical server log or displayed on my monitor. I'm not... I'm not hallucinating phantom coworkers or hearing whispers in the air vents. Everything I am telling you is grounded in tangible, verifiable, digital evidence. \n\nNow, as for the last part of your question—believing something strongly that others find hard to understand—well, sure, but you have to look at the context. People find it \"hard to understand\" because they lack the technical literacy, not because what I'm saying is some kind of fantasy. Take my manager, for example. If you sat him down and tried to explain a simple SQL injection or how to configure a basic firewall rule, his eyes would glaze over. He wouldn't understand a word of it. So when I sit in his office and show him the timestamp discrepancies in the ticket routing, of course he doesn't agree with me! He doesn't have the baseline knowledge to agree or disagree. He just trusts whatever the dashboard tells him to trust. \n\nIt's like... imagine trying to explain advanced calculus to a toddler, and when the toddler doesn't get it, someone accuses you of making up the math. That's what it feels like. The fact that management or HR finds my claims \"hard to understand\" isn't a reflection of my mental state, doctor, it's a reflection of their complete ignorance of network architecture. So, no, no strange sensations, no voices. Just a very real, very documented administrative problem that no one else in that building is qualified to see.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period lasting several days or longer where your mood was unusually high, irritable, or you felt extremely energetic, where you needed less sleep, or were more focused on getting things done than usual?\n\nPatient: Unusually high? Extremely energetic? No, no, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, I'm sixty years old, doctor. I haven't felt \"extremely energetic\" since the late nineties back when we were rolling out Windows 98 across three different corporate campuses. That took a lot of energy, let me tell you, we were working around the clock. But these days? No, my mood is... well, it's steady. I'm a steady, pragmatic kind of guy. \n\nAs for sleep, I absolutely need my sleep. I aim for a solid seven to eight hours every single night. Now, I will admit, lately, with everything going on at the office, with the constant need to double-check my ticket queues and document their little traps, I might toss and turn a bit. My wife will tell you I've been grinding my teeth, and sometimes it takes me an hour to wind down because my brain is still parsing through the day's server logs. But I'm not staying up for days on end, or feeling like I don't need to rest. I'm definitely not running on two hours of sleep. I leave that to the junior developers who survive on energy drinks and terrible coding habits. I know the value of pacing myself. \n\nAnd as for being more focused on getting things done... well, yes, I am focused, but not in some abnormal, unnatural way. It's just... when you're in my position, when you know there is a literal target on your back and a coordinated effort to push you out, you have to be focused. You have to dot every 'i' and cross every 't'. If I spend four uninterrupted hours meticulously reviewing a firewall configuration, it's not because I have some weird burst of energy, it's because if I miss one single parameter, they will use it as an excuse to write me up for a critical failure. So my focus is purely self-preservation, really. It's just dedication to my craft and protecting my livelihood from people who want to ruin it. So no, no strange mood swings or energy spikes. Just a guy trying to do his job, protect his reputation, and get a decent night's rest despite the circumstances.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period lasting two weeks or more where you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Down? Hopeless? No, I wouldn't use those words at all. I mean, sure, I get frustrated. Anyone would be frustrated when they’re dealing with the kind of systemic, targeted administrative lockouts I’m facing every single week. It’s aggravating, absolutely. You try trying to close out a tier-three support ticket when someone keeps remotely revoking your read-write permissions halfway through the process. But hopeless? Doctor, if I were hopeless, I would have just handed in my badge, packed up my desk, and let them win. I would have just rolled over and taken the early retirement package they keep trying to subtly push on me. \n\nBut I'm not giving up. I am actively fighting this. I'm documenting everything, I'm building a paper trail that even their highest-paid corporate lawyers won't be able to argue with. That takes drive. That takes determination, not hopelessness. \n\nAnd as for losing interest in things... no, not at all. When I’m not at the office, or, you know, when I’m not organizing my backup logs at home, I still do the things I’ve always loved. I have a pretty extensive home network setup in the basement—I run my own media server, I tinker with Raspberry Pi microcomputers, I’ve been getting into local home automation lately. Which, by the way, is a whole other can of worms when you start looking at the security protocols on these cheap smart devices, let me tell you. I have to isolate them on their own VLAN just so they don't phone home to some server overseas. But I enjoy that kind of problem-solving! \n\nAnd my wife and I, we still do our Sunday morning crosswords, we go to the farmer's market when the weather is nice... I still enjoy my life. It’s just that right now, a significant portion of my bandwidth—mentally speaking—has to be allocated to making sure my career isn’t dismantled by management. But that doesn't mean I'm sitting around crying or staring at the wall. I honestly don't have the time to be depressed. I have way too much work to do, both on the clock and off it, just to keep my head above water and prove exactly what they're doing to my system access. So, no, no two-week periods of feeling down or losing interest. I'm just highly occupied with defending myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief that you were absolutely certain was true, even when the people around you, including those who knew you well, strongly disagreed with you and provided clear evidence to the contrary?\n\nPatient: Belief I'm certain is true? Well, yes, but again, I have to challenge your phrasing there, doctor. \"Clear evidence to the contrary.\" That's... that's the crux of the whole issue right there. That is actually the entire reason I'm here in this office talking to you right now. \n\nFor over a year now—fourteen months, if we want to be exact, ever since the Q3 restructuring last year—I have been trying, practically begging, to get someone, anyone, to take this seriously. I am talking about the new ticket rotation system and these so-called \"random\" security audits they've been running. It is blindingly obvious to me what is happening. They are systematically using these audits, and the way they route the impossible, broken tickets specifically to my queue, to build a paper trail against me at work. It's a textbook constructive dismissal tactic. You flood the senior employee with unmanageable tasks, you audit their terminal constantly, and you document every minor, unavoidable delay as a performance failure so you can push them out without paying a proper severance. \n\nBut of course, when I bring this up, when I go to HR or my department head and lay it all out... they disagree. Strongly. Just like you said. They sit me down in these little glass-walled conference rooms, and they pull up the system logs on the projector, and they say, \"Look, it's just the new automated rotation. It's just policy. Everyone is getting audited, it's completely random.\" They point to the logs as their \"clear evidence to the contrary.\" \n\nBut doctor, I *know* how those logs are generated! I know how the routing algorithms are configured! I've been doing this for thirty-five years. A log only shows what the system parameters tell it to show. If you set the policy to disproportionately flag legacy accounts—like mine—then the log is going to look perfectly standard and random to an untrained HR rep, but it's completely skewed in reality. \n\nSo yes, I am absolutely certain this paper trail is being built to terminate me, and yes, management strongly disagrees and waves their little policy printouts in my face. But they aren't providing evidence to the contrary, they're just showing me the very tool they're using to do it! I just... I can't get anyone to look past their surface-level \"it's just policy\" excuse and see the actual, underlying architecture of what they're doing to me. It's maddening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever held a belief that others strongly disagreed with, but which you remained absolutely certain was true despite their disagreement and any evidence they provided?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I feel like we are going in circles here, because that is exactly what I just spent the last five minutes explaining to you. That is the entire reason I am sitting in this chair right now. I’m here because for over a year now—fourteen months, as I said—I’ve been trying to get someone, anyone with a shred of technical literacy, to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and these constant security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work. \n\nAnd yes, exactly to your point about people disagreeing and providing \"evidence,\" management keeps showing me logs and saying it’s just policy. I know the architecture. I know that if you tweak the load-balancing parameters even slightly on the backend, you can flood a specific user profile—mine—with all the corrupted, unresolvable legacy tickets while making it look completely automated to an auditor. So yes, I remain absolutely certain, because their so-called evidence is just a smokescreen. It’s a manipulated output designed to look like a neutral policy. I just don't know how else to explain it so that people will actually listen to the technical reality instead of just quoting the corporate handbook at me. It's like talking to a brick wall.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that events, objects, or the actions of others were specifically directed at you, even when there was no logical reason for it, outside of your workplace?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? You mean like... strangers on the street, or things happening in my neighborhood? No, no, definitely not. I mean, I'm a very rational person, doctor. I don't walk around thinking the universe revolves around me or that the cashier at the grocery store is somehow involved in a plot against me. When my wife and I go to the farmer's market, like I mentioned earlier, the guy who sells us the heirloom tomatoes—great guy, by the way, we talk about soil pH and irrigation setups for twenty minutes at a time—he's just selling tomatoes. They want to push out a senior employee with thirty-five years of salary accrual without paying the proper severance package. That's business. They pull me into these meetings, they slide a printout across the table—a literal piece of paper, if you can believe it, who prints out server logs in this day and age?—and they tap their fingers on it and say, \"Look, the algorithm assigned this batch, it's just standard policy, everyone goes through it.\" They expect me to just nod and accept that as objective proof. \n\nBut I wrote half the scripts they used to use before this new system! I know the architecture. I know that if you tweak the load-balancing parameters even slightly on the backend, you can flood a specific user profile—mine—with all the corrupted, unresolvable legacy tickets while making it look completely automated to an auditor. So yes, I remain absolutely certain, because their so-called evidence is just a smokescreen. It’s a manipulated output designed to look like a neutral policy. I just don't know how else to explain it so that people will actually listen to the technical reality instead of just quoting the corporate handbook at me. It's like talking to a brick wall.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that events, objects, or the actions of others were specifically directed at you, even when there was no logical reason for it, outside of your workplace?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? You mean like... strangers on the street, or things happening in my neighborhood? No, no, definitely not. I mean, I'm a very rational person, doctor. I don't walk around thinking the universe revolves around me or that the cashier at the grocery store is somehow involved in a plot against me. When my wife and I go to the farmer's market, like I mentioned earlier, the guy who sells us the heirloom tomatoes—great guy, by the way, we talk about soil pH and irrigation setups for twenty minutes at a time—he's just selling tomatoes. He's not secretly sending signals or whatever it is you're implying. \n\nHonestly, I think that's the clearest proof I have that what's happening at the office is a real, documented, targeted campaign. Because if I were just... I don't know, paranoid or losing my grip on reality, wouldn't I think everyone was out to get me? Wouldn't I think the mailman was spying on my house? But I don't. Bob, my neighbor on the left, we borrow each other's lawn tools all the time. The mailman is just delivering the mail. When I go down to Micro Center to pick up parts for my home server, the staff there are just kids trying to make an hourly wage, they aren't doing anything directed at me. \n\nMy life outside the office is completely normal. Boring, even, in a good way! It's just... logical. Everything has a logical reason. The only place where things are happening that are specifically directed at me is at work, and that's because there *is* a logical reason for it there. The motive is financial. They want to push out a senior employee with thirty-five years of salary accrual without paying the proper severance package. That's business. It's dirty business, but it makes logical sense from their bottom line. Out in the real world? No, nobody cares about me. I'm just a guy buying tomatoes and ethernet cables. So no, I don't feel like things outside of work are directed at me at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief that was not related to work that you were absolutely sure was true, even when the people around you, including those who knew you well, strongly disagreed and provided clear evidence that it wasn't true?\n\nPatient: I mean... doctor, again, I feel like you're searching for a pattern that just doesn't exist outside of that office building. I feel like we're splitting hairs here. If you're asking if I'm just generally a stubborn guy who ignores facts in my day-to-day life, the answer is no. Absolutely not. \n\nI don't have these... these irrational, unshakeable beliefs about my personal life, because my personal life isn't being manipulated by a corporate restructuring team. I'm a very logical man. If someone shows me real evidence, I listen to it. \n\nJust to give you an example—because I really need you to understand how I operate normally—last month, my wife and I had a disagreement. I was absolutely certain that the new mesh router I set up in the den, which is an ASUS dual-band, beautiful piece of hardware, I was convinced it was causing interference with our smart thermostat. I was sure of it, because the timing lined up perfectly with when I installed it. But my wife, she's a smart woman, she pulled up the connection logs on her tablet and showed me that the drop-offs were actually happening on a completely different 2.4 gigahertz frequency, coming from the neighbor's security camera setup. And you know what I did? I looked at the data—real, untampered data, not the garbage HR tries to feed me—and I said, \"You're right, Helen. I was wrong.\" \n\nThat is how normal people operate in the real world! When someone I trust shows me actual, objective evidence, I change my mind. I don't just blindly cling to things. \n\nSo no, I don't have anything like what you're describing outside of work. My life at home, my hobbies, my marriage... it's all grounded in reality. The only reason I am so unshakeable about what is happening at the office is because I know the backend architecture of their systems, and I know exactly how they are faking the evidence against me. It’s the Q3 restructuring, doctor. That's the anomaly here. It's not some overarching flaw in my psychology, it is a specific, documented campaign by management to avoid paying my severance. I really, really need you to see the distinction there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever believed that you were someone very important, had special abilities, or were meant to carry out a specific, grand mission that others did not recognize?\n\nPatient: Important? I don't have special abilities, unless you count being able to troubleshoot a defunct RAID array at three in the morning without waking up my wife, which, believe me, management certainly doesn't recognize as a 'special ability.' \n\nI really don't think I'm someone grand or... or meant for some historical, world-saving mission. I'm just a guy who has spent thirty-five years making sure networks don't collapse under the weight of corporate incompetence. That's not a grand mission, that's just the daily grind. I've never thought I was Steve Jobs or some tech visionary. I'm a nuts-and-bolts guy. I write scripts, I patch servers, I make sure the SQL databases don't corrupt during the nightly backups. It's practical work. It's almost blue-collar work, just done in a rolling chair. \n\nAnd honestly, if you think about it, that just proves my point about what management is doing right now. If I thought I was some incredibly important, irreplaceable genius, I wouldn't be sitting here telling you they're trying to push me out, would I? I'd be telling you they couldn't survive without me! But I know exactly what I am to them. I'm a line item. I'm a senior employee with a high salary and a massive severance package waiting for me if they lay me off legally. \n\nI'm not important to them as a person or a visionary, I'm just an expensive liability on their Q3 spreadsheet that they want to erase using these fake security audits. I know my place in the world, doctor. It's right there in the server room. So no, no grand missions here. Doctor, didn't... didn't we just cover this? I feel like we are going in circles here. I literally just told you about the router and my wife showing me the interference logs. So, no. Nothing unrelated to work. I really don't know how else to explain it to make you understand that I am completely grounded in reality in my day-to-day life. 

The entire reason I’m even sitting here in this chair talking to you right now is because for over a year now—fourteen months, if we're being exact, ever since the Q2 restructuring started—I’ve been trying to get someone, *anyone*, to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and these sudden security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work. A completely fabricated paper trail! 

And it drives me absolutely up the wall, because management keeps dragging me into these little ambush meetings, sliding printouts across the conference table, showing me system logs and saying, "Oh, it’s just policy, it's just standard procedure for the new compliance metrics." They show me these so-called clear pieces of evidence, and they expect me to just roll over and accept it because it looks official to an untrained eye. They bring in HR, they point at the spreadsheet, and they act like I'm the one being unreasonable for not accepting their "proof." 

But I know the backend architecture! I know how easy it is to alter a timestamp in the SQL database before exporting the log file. I know the Active Directory permissions they're using to route those doomed-to-fail tickets to my queue. So when people look at me like I'm being stubborn for not believing management's logs, it's not because I have some unshakeable, irrational belief. It's because I'm the only one who actually knows how the system is being manipulated to push me out before my pension vests. 

So, to answer your question again: no. Outside of work, if you show me real evidence, I accept it. I am a perfectly reasonable man. But I am not going to accept fake logs from a management team that is actively trying to ruin my thirty-five-year career.

A very deliberate, calculated paper trail. \n\nI've sat down with management, I don't know how many times at this point, to try and get them to take this seriously. And every single time, they just keep pulling up these server logs and pointing at the screen, telling me, \"Look, it's just policy, it's an automated algorithm assigning the tickets, it's standard procedure.\" But they don't seem to understand that I know exactly how you can manipulate the backend routing to target a specific user ID. They just keep smiling, showing me the exact same sanitized logs, and acting like it's just routine policy. It's exhausting, frankly, trying to get anyone to look at the actual reality of the situation when they just hide behind \"company policy\" while actively setting me up to fail.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that people at work are intentionally trying to harm you, set you up, or conspire against you in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, \"harm\"... I mean, if you're asking if someone is going to wait for me in the parking lot with a baseball bat, no, I'm not saying that. I'm a rational person, doctor. But professionally? Absolutely. \"Set me up\" is exactly the right phrase. \n\nYou see, when you've been in IT as long as I have—and we're talking about the early days, back when we were manually configuring IPX/SPX protocols, long before everything was just a neat little cloud-based dashboard—you learn how to read the digital footprints people leave behind. You see the patterns. And the pattern here is, frankly, undeniable. \n\nThey aren't just... it's not just some passive dislike or office politics. But if you look at the load balancing, I am consistently receiving the vast majority of the late-day criticals. And when I inevitably miss the SLA—the Service Level Agreement, which is our internal metric for response times—because it is physically impossible for one man to resolve multiple critical database failures simultaneously, what happens? It goes straight into my quarterly review. It gets flagged as a failure to meet basic performance metrics. \n\nSo, yes, they are setting me up. They want a documented, supposedly objective history of underperformance. And the most frustrating part of all of this, the part that really makes my blood boil, is that management just looks at these manufactured metrics and nods along. I've tried explaining the routing parameters to them, I've tried showing them how a simple script can bypass the randomizer to target my specific user ID, but they just glaze over. They don't want to hear it, because the setup gives them exactly what they want: a neat, tidy paper trail to eventually force me out. So yes, I absolutely believe they are conspiring against me. I don't just believe it, I can see the architecture of it.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your workplace, do you feel that other people in your life, like friends, family, or strangers, are also conspiring against you or trying to harm you in some way?\n\nPatient: No, no, not at all. If I see something, it's because it is right there in black and white, printed on a physical server log or displayed on my monitor. I'm not... I'm not hallucinating phantom coworkers or hearing whispers in the air vents. Everything I am telling you is grounded in tangible, verifiable, digital evidence. \n\nNow, as for the last part of your question—believing something strongly that others find hard to understand—well, sure, but you have to look at the context. People find it \"hard to understand\" because they lack the technical literacy, not because what I'm saying is some kind of fantasy. Take my manager, for example. If you sat him down and tried to explain a simple SQL injection or how to configure a basic firewall rule, his eyes would glaze over. He wouldn't understand a word of it. So when I sit in his office and show him the timestamp discrepancies in the ticket routing, of course he doesn't agree with me! He doesn't have the baseline knowledge to agree or disagree. He just trusts whatever the dashboard tells him to trust. \n\nIt's like... imagine trying to explain advanced calculus to a toddler, and when the toddler doesn't get it, someone accuses you of making up the math. That's what it feels like. The fact that management or HR finds my claims \"hard to understand\" isn't a reflection of my mental state, doctor, it's a reflection of their complete ignorance of network architecture. So, no, no strange sensations, no voices. Just a very real, very documented administrative problem that no one else in that building is qualified to see.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period lasting several days or longer where your mood was unusually high, irritable, or you felt extremely energetic, where you needed less sleep, or were more focused on getting things done than usual?\n\nPatient: Unusually high? Extremely energetic? No, no, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, I'm sixty years old, doctor. I haven't felt \"extremely energetic\" since the late nineties back when we were rolling out Windows 98 across three different corporate campuses. That took a lot of energy, let me tell you, we were working around the clock. But these days? No, my mood is... well, it's steady. I'm a steady, pragmatic kind of guy. \n\nAs for sleep, I absolutely need my sleep. I aim for a solid seven to eight hours every single night. Now, I will admit, lately, with everything going on at the office, with the constant need to double-check my ticket queues and document their little traps, I might toss and turn a bit. My wife will tell you I've been grinding my teeth, and sometimes it takes me an hour to wind down because my brain is still parsing through the day's server logs. But I'm not staying up for days on end, or feeling like I don't need to rest. I'm definitely not running on two hours of sleep. I leave that to the junior developers who survive on energy drinks and terrible coding habits. I know the value of pacing myself. \n\nAnd as for being more focused on getting things done... well, yes, I am focused, but not in some abnormal, unnatural way. It's just... when you're in my position, when you know there is a literal target on your back and a coordinated effort to push you out, you have to be focused. You have to dot every 'i' and cross every 't'. If I spend four uninterrupted hours meticulously reviewing a firewall configuration, it's not because I have some weird burst of energy, it's because if I miss one single parameter, they will use it as an excuse to write me up for a critical failure. So my focus is purely self-preservation, really. It's just dedication to my craft and protecting my livelihood from people who want to ruin it. So no, no strange mood swings or energy spikes. Just a guy trying to do his job, protect his reputation, and get a decent night's rest despite the circumstances.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period lasting two weeks or more where you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Down? Hopeless? No, I wouldn't use those words at all. I mean, sure, I get frustrated. Anyone would be frustrated when they’re dealing with the kind of systemic, targeted administrative lockouts I’m facing every single week. It’s aggravating, absolutely. You try trying to close out a tier-three support ticket when someone keeps remotely revoking your read-write permissions halfway through the process. But hopeless? Doctor, if I were hopeless, I would have just handed in my badge, packed up my desk, and let them win. I would have just rolled over and taken the early retirement package they keep trying to subtly push on me. \n\nBut I'm not giving up. I am actively fighting this. I'm documenting everything, I'm building a paper trail that even their highest-paid corporate lawyers won't be able to argue with. That takes drive. That takes determination, not hopelessness. \n\nAnd as for losing interest in things... no, not at all. When I’m not at the office, or, you know, when I’m not organizing my backup logs at home, I still do the things I’ve always loved. I have a pretty extensive home network setup in the basement—I run my own media server, I tinker with Raspberry Pi microcomputers, I’ve been getting into local home automation lately. Which, by the way, is a whole other can of worms when you start looking at the security protocols on these cheap smart devices, let me tell you. I have to isolate them on their own VLAN just so they don't phone home to some server overseas. But I enjoy that kind of problem-solving! \n\nAnd my wife and I, we still do our Sunday morning crosswords, we go to the farmer's market when the weather is nice... I still enjoy my life. It’s just that right now, a significant portion of my bandwidth—mentally speaking—has to be allocated to making sure my career isn’t dismantled by management. But that doesn't mean I'm sitting around crying or staring at the wall. I honestly don't have the time to be depressed. I have way too much work to do, both on the clock and off it, just to keep my head above water and prove exactly what they're doing to my system access. So, no, no two-week periods of feeling down or losing interest. I'm just highly occupied with defending myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief that you were absolutely certain was true, even when the people around you, including those who knew you well, strongly disagreed with you and provided clear evidence to the contrary?\n\nPatient: Belief I'm certain is true? Well, yes, but again, I have to challenge your phrasing there, doctor. \"Clear evidence to the contrary.\" That's... that's the crux of the whole issue right there. That is actually the entire reason I'm here in this office talking to you right now. \n\nFor over a year now—fourteen months, if we want to be exact, ever since the Q3 restructuring last year—I have been trying, practically begging, to get someone, anyone, to take this seriously. I am talking about the new ticket rotation system and these so-called \"random\" security audits they've been running. It is blindingly obvious to me what is happening. They are systematically using these audits, and the way they route the impossible, broken tickets specifically to my queue, to build a paper trail against me at work. It's a textbook constructive dismissal tactic. You flood the senior employee with unmanageable tasks, you audit their terminal constantly, and you document every minor, unavoidable delay as a performance failure so you can push them out without paying a proper severance. \n\nBut of course, when I bring this up, when I go to HR or my department head and lay it all out... they disagree. Strongly. Just like you said. They sit me down in these little glass-walled conference rooms, and they pull up the system logs on the projector, and they say, \"Look, it's just the new automated rotation. It's just policy. Everyone is getting audited, it's completely random.\" They point to the logs as their \"clear evidence to the contrary.\" \n\nBut doctor, I *know* how those logs are generated! I know how the routing algorithms are configured! I've been doing this for thirty-five years. A log only shows what the system parameters tell it to show. If you set the policy to disproportionately flag legacy accounts—like mine—then the log is going to look perfectly standard and random to an untrained HR rep, but it's completely skewed in reality. \n\nSo yes, I am absolutely certain this paper trail is being built to terminate me, and yes, management strongly disagrees and waves their little policy printouts in my face. But they aren't providing evidence to the contrary, they're just showing me the very tool they're using to do it! I just... I can't get anyone to look past their surface-level \"it's just policy\" excuse and see the actual, underlying architecture of what they're doing to me. It's maddening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever held a belief that others strongly disagreed with, but which you remained absolutely certain was true despite their disagreement and any evidence they provided?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I feel like we are going in circles here, because that is exactly what I just spent the last five minutes explaining to you. I know the architecture. I know that if you tweak the load-balancing parameters even slightly on the backend, you can flood a specific user profile—mine—with all the corrupted, unresolvable legacy tickets while making it look completely automated to an auditor. So yes, I remain absolutely certain, because their so-called evidence is just a smokescreen. It’s a manipulated output designed to look like a neutral policy. I just don't know how else to explain it so that people will actually listen to the technical reality instead of just quoting the corporate handbook at me. It's like talking to a brick wall.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that events, objects, or the actions of others were specifically directed at you, even when there was no logical reason for it, outside of your workplace?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? You mean like... strangers on the street, or things happening in my neighborhood? No, no, definitely not. I mean, I'm a very rational person, doctor. I don't walk around thinking the universe revolves around me or that the cashier at the grocery store is somehow involved in a plot against me. When my wife and I go to the farmer's market, like I mentioned earlier, the guy who sells us the heirloom tomatoes—great guy, by the way, we talk about soil pH and irrigation setups for twenty minutes at a time—he's just selling tomatoes. When I go down to Micro Center to pick up parts for my home server, the staff there are just kids trying to make an hourly wage, they aren't doing anything directed at me. \n\nMy life outside the office is completely normal. They want to push out a senior employee with thirty-five years of salary accrual without paying the proper severance package. That's business. So no, I don't feel like things outside of work are directed at me at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief that was not related to work that you were absolutely sure was true, even when the people around you, including those who knew you well, strongly disagreed and provided clear evidence that it wasn't true?\n\nPatient: I mean... doctor, again, I feel like you're searching for a pattern that just doesn't exist outside of that office building. Absolutely not. \n\nI don't have these... these irrational, unshakeable beliefs about my personal life, because my personal life isn't being manipulated by a corporate restructuring team. I was sure of it, because the timing lined up perfectly with when I installed it. I was wrong.\" \n\nThat is how normal people operate in the real world! When someone I trust shows me actual, objective evidence, I change my mind. I don't just blindly cling to things. \n\nSo no, I don't have anything like what you're describing outside of work. My life at home, my hobbies, my marriage... it's all grounded in reality. I mean, it doesn't take a genius, let alone someone with my technical background, to see what's really going on. They are using these systems to build a paper trail against me. And every single time, they just keep pulling up these server logs and pointing at the screen, telling me, \"Look, it's just policy, it's an automated algorithm assigning the tickets, it's standard procedure.\" But they don't seem to understand that I know exactly how you can manipulate the backend routing to target a specific user ID. They just keep smiling, showing me the exact same sanitized logs, and acting like it's just routine policy. It's exhausting, frankly, trying to get anyone to look at the actual reality of the situation when they just hide behind \"company policy\" while actively setting me up to fail.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that people at work are intentionally trying to harm you, set you up, or conspire against you in any way?\n\nPatient: Well, \"harm\"... I mean, if you're asking if someone is going to wait for me in the parking lot with a baseball bat, no, I'm not saying that. I'm a rational person, doctor. But professionally? Absolutely. \"Set me up\" is exactly the right phrase. \n\nYou see, when you've been in IT as long as I have—and we're talking about the early days, back when we were manually configuring IPX/SPX protocols, long before everything was just a neat little cloud-based dashboard—you learn how to read the digital footprints people leave behind. You see the patterns. And the pattern here is, frankly, undeniable. \n\nThey aren't just... it's not just some passive dislike or office politics. It's a highly coordinated effort to document a false narrative about my competence. I've thought about this a lot, analyzing the timestamps on the tickets I get assigned. For instance, I'll get a batch of priority-one server outage tickets right at 4:55 PM on a Friday. Now, the automated algorithm is supposed to distribute those evenly across the senior support team, right? There are several of us. But if you look at the load balancing, I am consistently receiving the vast majority of the late-day criticals. And when I inevitably miss the SLA—the Service Level Agreement, which is our internal metric for response times—because it is physically impossible for one man to resolve multiple critical database failures simultaneously, what happens? It goes straight into my quarterly review. It gets flagged as a failure to meet basic performance metrics. \n\nSo, yes, they are setting me up. They want a documented, supposedly objective history of underperformance. And the most frustrating part of all of this, the part that really makes my blood boil, is that management just looks at these manufactured metrics and nods along. They are completely ignorant—or willfully blind—to the backend manipulation it takes to generate those numbers. I've tried explaining the routing parameters to them, I've tried showing them how a simple script can bypass the randomizer to target my specific user ID, but they just glaze over. They don't want to hear it, because the setup gives them exactly what they want: a neat, tidy paper trail to eventually force me out. So yes, I absolutely believe they are conspiring against me. I don't just believe it, I can see the architecture of it.\n\nDoctor: Outside of your workplace, do you feel that other people in your life, like friends, family, or strangers, are also conspiring against you or trying to harm you in some way?\n\nPatient: No, no, not at all. It's strictly... it's confined entirely to the office. My family, my friends... they have nothing to do with this. I mean, my wife, she's been incredibly supportive through all of this, even though I know it's hard for her to grasp the technical nuances of what they're doing to the server logs. We've been married for thirty-five years, and she knows me, she knows my work ethic. She sees how stressed I am when I get home, how I have to spend hours just documenting everything that happened that day, taking screenshots of the ticket queues before they can scrub them, because you have to have your own backups, you know? \n\nAnd strangers? No, why would a stranger care about my SLA metrics? This isn't some grand, global conspiracy, doctor. I'm not... I'm not crazy. I'm not sitting here thinking the mailman is tapping my phone or the barista at the coffee shop is poisoning my espresso. That's absurd. This is a highly localized, professionally motivated campaign to remove me from my position without having to pay out a severance package or deal with a wrongful termination suit. \n\nIt's all about the money and the corporate restructuring they've been hinting at since Q3 of last year. They want the older, higher-salaried guys out so they can bring in these kids fresh out of a six-week coding bootcamp who don't know the difference between a subnet mask and a MAC address, but who will work for half the pay and never question management. That's all this is. It's just business, but it's dirty business. So, no, my personal life is fine, or at least, as fine as it can be when you're dealing with this level of professional sabotage for eight to ten hours a day. It's just the people at the office. They're the ones with the motive, the means, and the administrative access to pull this off.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any strange or unusual thoughts, sensations, or perceptions that other people don’t seem to notice or agree with, such as hearing voices, seeing things that others don’t, or believing something strongly that others find hard to understand?\n\nPatient: Hearing voices? Seeing things? No, no, let me stop you right there, doctor. Absolutely not. I think... I think you might be fundamentally misunderstanding the nature of what I'm dealing with here. \n\nI work in IT. My entire career, my entire adult life, has been built on hard data. Zeros and ones. Binary realities. If I see something, it's because it is right there in black and white, printed on a physical server log or displayed on my monitor. I'm not... I'm not hallucinating phantom coworkers or hearing whispers in the air vents. Everything I am telling you is grounded in tangible, verifiable, digital evidence. \n\nNow, as for the last part of your question—believing something strongly that others find hard to understand—well, sure, but you have to look at the context. People find it \"hard to understand\" because they lack the technical literacy, not because what I'm saying is some kind of fantasy. Take my manager, for example. If you sat him down and tried to explain a simple SQL injection or how to configure a basic firewall rule, his eyes would glaze over. He wouldn't understand a word of it. So when I sit in his office and show him the timestamp discrepancies in the ticket routing, of course he doesn't agree with me! He doesn't have the baseline knowledge to agree or disagree. He just trusts whatever the dashboard tells him to trust. \n\nIt's like... imagine trying to explain advanced calculus to a toddler, and when the toddler doesn't get it, someone accuses you of making up the math. That's what it feels like. The fact that management or HR finds my claims \"hard to understand\" isn't a reflection of my mental state, doctor, it's a reflection of their complete ignorance of network architecture. So, no, no strange sensations, no voices. Just a very real, very documented administrative problem that no one else in that building is qualified to see.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period lasting several days or longer where your mood was unusually high, irritable, or you felt extremely energetic, where you needed less sleep, or were more focused on getting things done than usual?\n\nPatient: Unusually high? Extremely energetic? No, no, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, I'm sixty years old, doctor. I haven't felt \"extremely energetic\" since the late nineties back when we were rolling out Windows 98 across three different corporate campuses. That took a lot of energy, let me tell you, we were working around the clock. But these days? No, my mood is... well, it's steady. I'm a steady, pragmatic kind of guy. \n\nAs for sleep, I absolutely need my sleep. I aim for a solid seven to eight hours every single night. Now, I will admit, lately, with everything going on at the office, with the constant need to double-check my ticket queues and document their little traps, I might toss and turn a bit. My wife will tell you I've been grinding my teeth, and sometimes it takes me an hour to wind down because my brain is still parsing through the day's server logs. But I'm not staying up for days on end, or feeling like I don't need to rest. I'm definitely not running on two hours of sleep. I leave that to the junior developers who survive on energy drinks and terrible coding habits. I know the value of pacing myself. \n\nAnd as for being more focused on getting things done... well, yes, I am focused, but not in some abnormal, unnatural way. It's just... when you're in my position, when you know there is a literal target on your back and a coordinated effort to push you out, you have to be focused. You have to dot every 'i' and cross every 't'. If I spend four uninterrupted hours meticulously reviewing a firewall configuration, it's not because I have some weird burst of energy, it's because if I miss one single parameter, they will use it as an excuse to write me up for a critical failure. So my focus is purely self-preservation, really. It's just dedication to my craft and protecting my livelihood from people who want to ruin it. So no, no strange mood swings or energy spikes. Just a guy trying to do his job, protect his reputation, and get a decent night's rest despite the circumstances.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period lasting two weeks or more where you felt down, hopeless, or lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Down? Hopeless? No, I wouldn't use those words at all. I mean, sure, I get frustrated. Anyone would be frustrated when they’re dealing with the kind of systemic, targeted administrative lockouts I’m facing every single week. It’s aggravating, absolutely. You try trying to close out a tier-three support ticket when someone keeps remotely revoking your read-write permissions halfway through the process. But hopeless? Doctor, if I were hopeless, I would have just handed in my badge, packed up my desk, and let them win. I would have just rolled over and taken the early retirement package they keep trying to subtly push on me. \n\nBut I'm not giving up. I am actively fighting this. I'm documenting everything, I'm building a paper trail that even their highest-paid corporate lawyers won't be able to argue with. That takes drive. That takes determination, not hopelessness. \n\nAnd as for losing interest in things... no, not at all. When I’m not at the office, or, you know, when I’m not organizing my backup logs at home, I still do the things I’ve always loved. I have a pretty extensive home network setup in the basement—I run my own media server, I tinker with Raspberry Pi microcomputers, I’ve been getting into local home automation lately. Which, by the way, is a whole other can of worms when you start looking at the security protocols on these cheap smart devices, let me tell you. I have to isolate them on their own VLAN just so they don't phone home to some server overseas. But I enjoy that kind of problem-solving! \n\nAnd my wife and I, we still do our Sunday morning crosswords, we go to the farmer's market when the weather is nice... I still enjoy my life. It’s just that right now, a significant portion of my bandwidth—mentally speaking—has to be allocated to making sure my career isn’t dismantled by management. But that doesn't mean I'm sitting around crying or staring at the wall. I honestly don't have the time to be depressed. I have way too much work to do, both on the clock and off it, just to keep my head above water and prove exactly what they're doing to my system access. So, no, no two-week periods of feeling down or losing interest. I'm just highly occupied with defending myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief that you were absolutely certain was true, even when the people around you, including those who knew you well, strongly disagreed with you and provided clear evidence to the contrary?\n\nPatient: Belief I'm certain is true? Well, yes, but again, I have to challenge your phrasing there, doctor. \"Clear evidence to the contrary.\" That's... that's the crux of the whole issue right there. That is actually the entire reason I'm here in this office talking to you right now. \n\nFor over a year now—fourteen months, if we want to be exact, ever since the Q3 restructuring last year—I have been trying, practically begging, to get someone, anyone, to take this seriously. I am talking about the new ticket rotation system and these so-called \"random\" security audits they've been running. It is blindingly obvious to me what is happening. They are systematically using these audits, and the way they route the impossible, broken tickets specifically to my queue, to build a paper trail against me at work. It's a textbook constructive dismissal tactic. You flood the senior employee with unmanageable tasks, you audit their terminal constantly, and you document every minor, unavoidable delay as a performance failure so you can push them out without paying a proper severance. \n\nBut of course, when I bring this up, when I go to HR or my department head and lay it all out... they disagree. Strongly. Just like you said. They sit me down in these little glass-walled conference rooms, and they pull up the system logs on the projector, and they say, \"Look, it's just the new automated rotation. It's just policy. Everyone is getting audited, it's completely random.\" They point to the logs as their \"clear evidence to the contrary.\" \n\nBut doctor, I *know* how those logs are generated! I know how the routing algorithms are configured! I've been doing this for thirty-five years. A log only shows what the system parameters tell it to show. If you set the policy to disproportionately flag legacy accounts—like mine—then the log is going to look perfectly standard and random to an untrained HR rep, but it's completely skewed in reality. \n\nSo yes, I am absolutely certain this paper trail is being built to terminate me, and yes, management strongly disagrees and waves their little policy printouts in my face. But they aren't providing evidence to the contrary, they're just showing me the very tool they're using to do it! I just... I can't get anyone to look past their surface-level \"it's just policy\" excuse and see the actual, underlying architecture of what they're doing to me. It's maddening.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever held a belief that others strongly disagreed with, but which you remained absolutely certain was true despite their disagreement and any evidence they provided?\n\nPatient: Doctor, I feel like we are going in circles here, because that is exactly what I just spent the last five minutes explaining to you. That is the entire reason I am sitting in this chair right now. I’m here because for over a year now—fourteen months, as I said—I’ve been trying to get someone, anyone with a shred of technical literacy, to take seriously that the new ticket rotation and these constant security audits are being used to build a paper trail against me at work. \n\nAnd yes, exactly to your point about people disagreeing and providing \"evidence,\" management keeps showing me logs and saying it’s just policy. I know the architecture. I know that if you tweak the load-balancing parameters even slightly on the backend, you can flood a specific user profile—mine—with all the corrupted, unresolvable legacy tickets while making it look completely automated to an auditor. So yes, I remain absolutely certain, because their so-called evidence is just a smokescreen. It’s a manipulated output designed to look like a neutral policy. I just don't know how else to explain it so that people will actually listen to the technical reality instead of just quoting the corporate handbook at me. It's like talking to a brick wall.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that events, objects, or the actions of others were specifically directed at you, even when there was no logical reason for it, outside of your workplace?\n\nPatient: Outside of work? You mean like... strangers on the street, or things happening in my neighborhood? No, no, definitely not. I mean, I'm a very rational person, doctor. I don't walk around thinking the universe revolves around me or that the cashier at the grocery store is somehow involved in a plot against me. When my wife and I go to the farmer's market, like I mentioned earlier, the guy who sells us the heirloom tomatoes—great guy, by the way, we talk about soil pH and irrigation setups for twenty minutes at a time—he's just selling tomatoes. He's not secretly sending signals or whatever it is you're implying. \n\nHonestly, I think that's the clearest proof I have that what's happening at the office is a real, documented, targeted campaign. Boring, even, in a good way! It's just... logical. Everything has a logical reason. They want to push out a senior employee with thirty-five years of salary accrual without paying the proper severance package. That's business. It's dirty business, but it makes logical sense from their bottom line. Out in the real world? No, nobody cares about me. I'm just a guy buying tomatoes and ethernet cables. So no, I don't feel like things outside of work are directed at me at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a belief that was not related to work that you were absolutely sure was true, even when the people around you, including those who knew you well, strongly disagreed and provided clear evidence that it wasn't true?\n\nPatient: I mean... doctor, again, I feel like you're searching for a pattern that just doesn't exist outside of that office building. Absolutely not. \n\nI don't have these... these irrational, unshakeable beliefs about my personal life, because my personal life isn't being manipulated by a corporate restructuring team. I was sure of it, because the timing lined up perfectly with when I installed it. I don't just blindly cling to things. \n\nSo no, I don't have anything like what you're describing outside of work. My life at home, my hobbies, my marriage... it's all grounded in reality.
